Global smooth solutions of 2D quasilinear wave equations with higher order null conditions and short pulse initial data
Ding Bingbing, Xin Zhouping, Yin Huicheng 1. School of Mathematical Sciences and Mathematical Institute, Nanjing Normal University,Nanjing, 210023, China. 2. Institute of Mathematical Sciences, The Chinese University of Hong Kong, Shatin, NT, Hong Kong.

Abstract




Abstract

For the short pulse initial data
with a first order outgoing constraint condition and optimal orders of smallness, we establish the global existence of smooth solutions
to 2D quasilinear wave equations with higher order null conditions. Such kinds of wave equations
include 2D relativistic membrane equations, 2D membrane equations,
and some 2D quasilinear equations which come
from the nonlinear Maxwell equations in electromagnetic theory or
from the corresponding Lagrangian functionals as perturbations of the Lagrangian densities of linear wave operators. The main ingredients of the analysis here include looking for a new good unknown, finding some key identities based on the higher order null conditions and the resulting null frames, as well as overcoming the difficulties due to the slow decay of solutions to the 2-D wave equation, so that the solutions can be estimated precisely.

Keywords:
Quasilinear wave equations, short pulse initial data, higher order null condition,

inverse foliation density, good unknown, null frame

Mathematical Subject Classification:
35L05, 35L72

1
Introduction

1.1
The problem and main results

Consider the 2D quasilinear wave equation

\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{2}g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\phi=0

(1.1)

with the short pulse initial data

\displaystyle\phi|_{t=1}=\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}\phi_{0}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega),\ \partial_{t}\phi|_{t=1}=\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\phi_{1}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega),

(1.2)

where
(x^{0},x)=(t,x^{1},x^{2})\in[1,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\partial=(\partial_{0},\partial_{1},\partial_{2})=(\partial_{x^{0}},\partial_{x^{1}},\partial_{x^{2}})
,

g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)=g^{\beta\alpha}(\partial\phi)
are smooth
functions of their arguments,
\delta>0
is small,
0<\varepsilon_{0}<1
is a fixed constant,
r=|x|=\sqrt{(x^{1})^{2}+(x^{2})^{2}}
,

\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\frac{x}{r}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
,
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})(s,\omega)
are smooth functions defined
in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}

with compact support in
(-1,0)
for the variable
s
. It is pointed out that when
\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{1}{2}
in (
1.2
),
such a class of initial data are first introduced by D. Christodoulou
[
7
]

for studying the formation of trapped surfaces in the Einstein vacuum spacetime
(see also
[
16
]
).

In this paper, we assume that for fixed integer
k\geq 2
, it holds that

g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)=m^{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{0\leq\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{k}\leq 2}g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{k}}\partial_{\gamma_{1}}\phi\cdots\partial_{\gamma_{k}}\phi+h^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)

(1.3)

for small
\partial\phi
,
where
m^{00}=-1
,
m^{ii}=1
for
1\leq i\leq 2
,
m^{\alpha\beta}=0
for
\alpha\neq\beta
,
the constants
g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{k}}
are equal for all the permutations of
(\alpha,\beta)
and
(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{k})
respectively,
and
h^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)=O(|\partial\phi|^{k+1})
.

A lot of physical or geometric models admit the form (
1.3
),
which include:

(1) The 2D relativistic membrane equation (corresponding to
k=2
in (
1.3
))

\partial_{t}\big(\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{t}\phi}{\sqrt{1-(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}}}\big)-div\big(\displaystyle\frac{\nabla_{x}\phi}{\sqrt{1-(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}}}\big)=0\quad\text{with $\nabla_{x}\phi=(\partial_{1}\phi,\partial_{2}\phi)$}.

(1.4)

Note that (
1.4
) is the Euler-Lagrange equation of the area functional

\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sqrt{1+|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}-(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}}dtdx

for the embedding of
(t,x)\to(t,x,\phi(t,x))
in the Minkowski spacetime.

(2) The 2D nonlinear membrane equation (corresponding to
k=2
in (
1.3
))

\partial_{t}^{2}\phi-div\big(\displaystyle\frac{\nabla_{x}\phi}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}}}\big)=0,

(1.5)

where
\phi(t,x)
stands for the position of membrane at point
(t,x)
.

(3) The nonlinear wave equation (corresponding to
k=p
in (
1.3
),
p\in\mathbb{N}
)

-(1+(\partial_{t}\phi)^{p+1})\partial_{t}^{2}\phi+\Delta\phi=0,

(1.6)

which comes from the variation of the Lagrangian functional

L(\phi)=-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}-\frac{(\partial_{t}\phi)^{p+3}}{(p+3)(p+2)}.

Especially, (
1.6
) with
p=1
may be regarded as a model equation from the nonlinear
version of Maxwell equations in the electromagnetic theory (see
[
22
, Section 1.3]
);

(4) The nonlinear wave equation (corresponding to
k=2p
in (
1.3
),
p\in\mathbb{N}
)

-\partial_{t}^{2}\phi+\Delta\phi-\partial_{t}\bigg(\big(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}\big)^{p}\partial_{t}\phi\bigg)+div\bigg(\big(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}\big)^{p}\nabla_{x}\phi\bigg)=0,

(1.7)

which is the Euler-Lagrangian equation of

L(\phi)=-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}+\frac{1}{p+1}\big(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}\big)^{p+1}
, which can be thought as a
suitable perturbation of the Lagrangian density of the linear wave operator.

(5) As a generalization of (
1.6
) and (
1.7
), the nonlinear wave
equation (corresponding to
k=2p
in (
1.3
),
p\in\mathbb{N}
)

\begin{split}&-(1+(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2p+1})\partial_{t}^{2}\phi+\Delta\phi-\partial_{t}\bigg(\big(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}\big)^{p}\partial_{t}\phi\bigg)\\
&+div\bigg(\big(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}\big)^{p}\nabla_{x}\phi\bigg)=0\end{split}

(1.8)

satisfies the
(2p)^{\text{th}}
null condition but violates the
(2p+1)^{\text{th}}
(see the definition of the
k^{\text{th}}

null condition in (
1.9
)). Note that
(
1.8
) corresponds to the Euler-Lagrangian equation of

L(\phi)=-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}+\frac{1}{p+1}\big(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}\big)^{p+1}-\frac{(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2p+3}}{(2p+3)(2p+2)}\quad(p\in\mathbb{N}).

For the study of the global existence of smooth solutions to (
1.1
)
with (
1.3
), it is often crucial to introduce the following definition of the
k^{\text{th}}

null condition
(k\geq 2,k\in\mathbb{N})

for (
1.1
):

\sum_{0\leq\alpha,\beta,\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{k}\leq 2}g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{k}}\xi_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}\xi_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots{\xi_{\gamma_{k}}}\equiv 0\quad\text{holds for $\xi_{0}=-1$ and
$(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\mathbb{S}^{1}$}.

(1.9)

From now on, the
k^{\text{th}}
null condition with
k\geq 2
will be called higher order null condition in this paper.

We emphasize that for small
\partial\phi
and general
g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)
with

\displaystyle g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)=m^{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{0\leq\gamma\leq 2}g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\phi+\sum_{0\leq\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\leq 2}g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}\partial_{\gamma_{1}}\phi\partial_{\gamma_{2}}\phi+O(|\partial\phi|^{3}),

(1.10)

the first and second null conditions have been introduced for (
1.1
)
(see
[
5
,
15
,
3
,
4
]
), namely,
there hold that for
\xi_{0}=-1
and
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq\alpha,\beta,\gamma\leq 2}g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\xi_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}\xi_{\gamma}\equiv 0\quad\text{and}\sum_{0\leq\alpha,\beta,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\leq 2}g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}\xi_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}\xi_{\gamma_{1}}\xi_{\gamma_{2}}\equiv 0,\quad\text{respectively.}

(1.11)

Moreover, it has been shown that for small smooth initial data with compact supports

\displaystyle\phi|_{t=1}=\delta\psi_{0}(x),\ \partial_{t}\phi|_{t=1}=\delta\psi_{1}(x),\ (\psi_{0}(x),\psi_{1}(x))\not\equiv 0,

(1.12)

(
1.1
) with (
1.10
) and (
1.12
) admits
a global smooth solution
\phi
if and only if (
1.11
) holds true,
otherwise, the solution
\phi
may blow up in finite
time and further the shock is formed (see
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
8
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
20
,
23
,
24
]
).

For 2D potential equation of irrotational isentropic
Chaplygin gases

\begin{split}&\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{2}g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\phi=-\partial_{t}^{2}\phi+\triangle\phi-2\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{t}\partial_{i}\phi+2\partial_{t}\phi\triangle\phi-\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\partial_{ij}^{2}\phi+|\nabla\phi|^{2}\triangle\phi=0,\end{split}

(1.13)

whose coefficients fulfill (
1.11
).
Under the short pulse initial data (
1.2
) and the first order outgoing constraint condition

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})\phi|_{t=1}=O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}),

(1.14)

it is shown in
[
11
]
that when
0<\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{1}{2}
, (
1.13
) has a global smooth solution
\phi

with
|\partial\phi|\leq C\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{-1/2}
for all
t\geq 1
.
As illustrated in
[
11
]
, in order to keep the strict hyperbolicity of (
1.1
) or (
1.13
),
the smallness of
\partial\phi
is certainly required
and can be derived from (
1.2
) and (
1.14
).

Motivated by the results of
[
22
,
9
,
19
]
in 3D case, we expect to find the optimal order
\varepsilon_{k}^{*}

of smallness in (
1.2
) such that if (
1.3
) satisfies the
k^{\text{th}}
null condition
but does not fulfill the
(k+1)^{\text{th}}
null condition, then the smooth solution of (
1.1
) with (
1.2
) and (
1.14
)
exists globally when
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,\varepsilon_{k}^{*})
and may blow up in finite time when
\varepsilon_{0}\in[\varepsilon_{k}^{*},1)
.

Note that it follows easily from the forms of (
1.2
) and (
1.14
) that for
0\leq p\leq 1
,

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})^{p}\Omega^{j}\partial^{\alpha}\phi|_{t=1}=O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|\alpha|}),

(1.15)

where
\Omega=x^{1}\partial_{2}-x^{2}\partial_{1}
. Without loss of generality and for simplicity, it is assumed that in (
1.1
),

g^{00}(\partial\phi)=-1.

(1.16)

Our main result in the paper is:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\varepsilon_{k}^{*}=\frac{k}{k+1}
with
k\geq 2
.
Under the conditions (
1.3
), (
1.9
), (
1.14
) and (
1.16
),
it holds that for small
\delta>0
,
when
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,\varepsilon_{k}^{*})
, (
1.1
) with (
1.2
)
admits a global smooth solution
\phi
which satisfies

\phi\in C^{\infty}([1,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\text{$|\partial\phi|\leq C\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{-1/2}$
and $|\phi|\leq C\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{1/2}$ for $t\geq 1$,}

where
C>0
is a uniform constant independent of
\delta
and
\varepsilon_{0}
.

Remark 1.1
.

As a direct application of Theorem
1.1
, (
1.6
) with
p=k\geq 2
has a global solution when the initial data are identical to those in Theorem
1.1
and
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,\varepsilon_{k}^{*})
. Conversely,
for certain initial short pulse data, it follows from
[
19
]
that the solution of (
1.6
) with
p=k\geq 1
may blow up
in finite time when
\varepsilon_{0}\in[\varepsilon_{k}^{*},1)
.
As previously mentioned, (
1.6
) with
p=k
is a special equation satisfying the
k^{\text{th}}

null condition but violates the
{(k+1)}^{\text{th}}
null condition. Therefore, we conjecture that, for
\varepsilon_{0}\in[\varepsilon_{k}^{*},1)
with
k\geq 2
, if

h^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)=\sum_{0\leq\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{k+1}\leq 2}h^{\alpha\beta,\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{k+1}}(\partial_{\gamma_{1}}\phi)\cdots(\partial_{\gamma_{k+1}}\phi)+O(|\partial\phi|^{k+2})
in (
1.3
)
and
\sum_{0\leq\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{k+1}\leq 2}h^{\alpha\beta,\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{k+1}}\xi_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}\xi_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots{\xi_{\gamma_{k+1}}}\not\equiv 0
for
\xi_{0}=-1
and
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, then there exist a class of initial data specified by (
1.2
) such that the smooth solution of (
1.1
) will undergo finite-time blowup.
This intriguing phenomenon will be one of the topics we intend to explore in our future research.

On the other hand, it is easy to verify that (
1.4
) satisfies the second order null condition but (
1.5
) does not,
(
1.7
) satisfies the
(2p)^{\text{th}}
null condition, (
1.8
) fulfills the
(2p)^{\text{th}}
null condition
but does not fulfills the
(2p+1)^{\text{th}}
null condition.
Collecting Theorem
1.1
and the analogous arguments in
[
22
,
9
,
19
]

as for (
1.6
) with (
1.2
) and (
1.14
), we have

Corollary 1.1
.

Under the conditions (
1.2
) and (
1.15
),

(1) (
1.4
) has a global smooth solution
\phi
when
0<\varepsilon_{0}<\varepsilon_{2}^{*}=\frac{2}{3}
.

(2) the smooth solution
\phi
of (
1.7
) exists globally when
0<\varepsilon_{0}<\varepsilon_{2p}^{*}=\frac{2p}{2p+1}
.

(3) assume that there exists a point
(s_{0},\omega_{0})\in(-1,0)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
such that

\begin{split}&\partial_{s}\phi_{0}(s_{0},\omega_{0})\partial_{s}^{2}\phi_{0}(s_{0},\omega_{0})>1\qquad\text{for $\varepsilon_{1}^{*}=\frac{1}{2}<\varepsilon_{0}<1$},\\
&\partial_{s}\phi_{0}(s_{0},\omega_{0})\partial_{s}^{2}\phi_{0}(s_{0},\omega_{0})>2\qquad\text{for $\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{1}^{*}$},\end{split}

(1.17)

the smooth solution
\phi
of (
1.5
) will blow up in finite time and further the shock is formed.

(4) when
0<\varepsilon_{0}<\varepsilon_{2p}^{*}=\frac{2p}{2p+1}
, the smooth solution
\phi
of (
1.8
) exists globally;
when
\varepsilon_{2p}^{*}\leq\varepsilon_{0}<1
, if there exists a point
(s_{0},\omega_{0})\in(-1,0)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
such that

\begin{split}&\phi_{1}^{2p}(s_{0},\omega_{0})\partial_{s}\phi_{1}(s_{0},\omega_{0})>\frac{2}{2p+1}\qquad\text{for $\varepsilon_{2p}^{*}<\varepsilon_{0}<1$},\\
&\phi_{1}^{2p}(s_{0},\omega_{0})\partial_{s}\phi_{1}(s_{0},\omega_{0})>\frac{(p+1)2^{2p+1}}{(2^{2p}-1)(2p+1)}\qquad\text{for $\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{2p}^{*}$},\end{split}

(1.18)

then the smooth solution
\phi
of (
1.8
) can blow up in finite time and further the shock is formed.

1.2
Some remarks

Remark 1.2
.

Note that
(
1.15
) can be easily fulfilled for any given smooth function
\phi_{0}

and the choice of
\phi_{1}(s,\omega)=-\partial_{s}\phi_{0}(s,\omega)
.

Remark 1.3
.

Under the outgoing constraint conditions (
1.15
) with large enough
p
and some number
\varepsilon_{0}
, for the short pulse initial data,
the authors in
[
21
,
25
]
have
established the existence of global smooth solutions
for the 3D semi-linear wave system satisfying the first null condition
and for
n
-dimensional (
n=2,3
) relativistic membrane equations, respectively. Note that, as illustrated in
[
11
]
, the largeness of
p
and some suitable
\varepsilon_{0}
for (
1.15
)
play the key roles in the arguments of
[
21
,
25
]
.
Note that the equation of the form (
1.1
) studied in
[
25
]
is a special case with
k=2

and
h^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)\equiv 0
in (
1.9
). In addition, the particular divergence structure in
[
25
]
makes it possible to introduce an elaborate vector field which approximates
\partial_{t}+\partial_{r}
effectively and to obtain the global existence of the solution with the help of the method in
[
21
]
. However, it seems difficult to adopt such an approach in
[
21
,
25
]
to treat the general (
1.1
) with the short pulse initial data (
1.2
) under the weaker outgoing constraint condition (
1.14
) and the optimal scope of
\varepsilon_{0}
. On the other hand, some recent progress on the global large data solutions of (
1.4
)
can be referred to
[
1
]
and
[
18
]
.

Remark 1.4
.

Our analysis is strongly motivated by the geometric approach pioneered by D. Christodoulou
[
6
]
, whose original purpose was to study the formation of shocks in multi-dimensional hyperbolic systems with genuinely nonlinear conditions, and subsequently for second order wave equations that do not satisfy the corresponding null conditions. See also
[
8
,
12
,
20
,
22
,
23
,
24
]
for further details.

Remark 1.5
.

Compared to
[
10
]
and
[
11
]
, the main new difficulty in this paper is how to get the precise estimates of related quantities which makes possible to get global solution when
\varepsilon_{0}
approaches
\varepsilon_{k}^{*}
. To this end, near the outgoing light cone surface, by identifying new suitable unknown variables (e.g. (
3.177
), (
4.221
), (
4.222
)) and utilizing some fundamental identities (e.g. (
3
), (
4.207
)) that arise under the higher-order null conditions and the corresponding null frames, we are able to estimate accurately the solution and determine the optimal smallness exponent for the short pulse initial data (for example, the higher order null conditions help us rewrite
(\partial_{\varphi_{\gamma}}g^{\alpha\beta})\mathring{L}_{\alpha}\mathring{L}_{\beta}\mathring{L}_{\gamma}
in (
2.60
) as (
3
), and the resulting good unknown
\mathscr{A}
is defined in (
3.177
). The dedicate estimate of
\mathscr{A}
in (
3.192
) yields (
4.287
) with the aid of (
2.36
), which is crucial for closing energy estimates in (
5.302
) and (
5.303
) when
\varepsilon_{0}
approaches
\varepsilon_{k}^{*}
). Within the interior of the outgoing light cone, based on precise boundary estimates of the solution on
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}=\{(t,x):t\geq t_{0},t-r=2\delta\}
(see Proposition
6.1
) due to the condition
0<\varepsilon_{0}<\varepsilon_{k}^{*}
, we derive global spacetime weighted energy estimates for the related Goursat problem.

1.3
Sketch of the proof and some notations

We now sketch the proof of Theorem
1.1
.
As in
[
10
,
11
]
, the proof of Theorem 1.1 relies on some a priori uniform energy estimates and the continuous induction
argument. Let
A_{2\delta}=\{(t,x):t\geq 1+2\delta,0\leq t-r\leq 2\delta\}

and
B_{2\delta}=\{(t,x):t\geq 1+2\delta,t-r\geq 2\delta\}
, which are near the outermost outgoing conic surface
C_{0}=\{(t,x):t\geq 1+2\delta,t=r\}

and inside the light cone
\{(t,x):t\geq 1+2\delta,t\geq r\}
, respectively.
Based on the local existence of solution
\phi
with some desired properties
for
t\in[1,1+2\delta]
, both the global uniform energy estimates
in
A_{2\delta}
and
B_{2\delta}
will be established.

We start with estimates of the solution
\phi
of (
1.1
) in
A_{2\delta}
.
Using the geometric framework of D. Christodoulou in
[
6
]
,
we shall prove that the outgoing characteristic conic surfaces never intersect as in
[
10
,
11
]
.
To this end,
as in
[
6
,
23
]
, the
“optical function”

u
of (
1.1
) can be defined as

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{2}g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)\partial_{\alpha}{u}\partial_{\beta}{u}=0,\\
&u(1+2\delta,x)=1+2\delta-r,\end{aligned}\right.

(1.19)

and subsequently the inverse foliation density is

\mu=-\displaystyle(\sum_{\alpha=0}^{2}g^{\alpha 0}\partial_{\alpha}u)^{-1},

(1.20)

where
g^{\alpha\beta}=g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)
. Set the metric
g=\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{2}g_{\alpha\beta}dx^{\alpha}dx^{\beta}

with
(g_{\alpha\beta})
being the inverse matrix of
(g^{\alpha\beta})
and

\mathring{L}=-\mu\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{2}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\partial_{\beta}.

(1.21)

Extend the local coordinate
\theta
on the standard circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
by

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathring{L}\vartheta=0,\\
&\vartheta|_{t=1+2\delta}=\theta.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.22)

As in
[
23
,
22
]
, perform the change of coordinates:

(t,x^{1},x^{2})\longrightarrow(s,u,\vartheta)
near
C_{0}
with

\begin{array}[]{l}s=t,\quad u=u(t,x),\quad\vartheta=\vartheta(t,x).\\
\end{array}

(1.23)

Then
X:=\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta}
is a tangent vector on the curved circle
S_{s,u}:=\{(s^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta):s^{\prime}=s,u^{\prime}=u\}
.
Under the suitable bootstrap assumptions on the smallness and time decay rate of
\partial\phi
(see
(\star)

in Section
2.2
), we will show that the inverse foliation density

\mu
satisfies
|\mathring{L}\mu|\lesssim\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-(k+2)/2}\mu+\delta^{k-(k+1)\varepsilon_{0}}s^{-(k+1)/2}
.
By
\mu(1+2\delta,x)\sim 1
and through the integration along integral curves of
\mathring{L}
,
\mu\sim 1
can
be derived for small
\delta>0
and
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,\varepsilon_{k}^{*})
. The positivity of
\mu
implies that the outgoing characteristic conic surfaces never
intersect as long as the smooth solution
\phi
to (
1.1
) with (
1.2
)
exists. Set
\varphi=(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}):=(\partial_{t}\phi,\partial_{1}\phi,\partial_{2}\phi)
.
Then
\varphi
solves a quasilinear wave system:

\mu\Box_{g}\varphi_{\gamma}=F_{\gamma}(\varphi,\partial\varphi),\quad\gamma=0,1,2,

(1.24)

where
\Box_{g}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det g}}\partial_{\alpha}(\sqrt{\det g}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta})
, and
F_{\gamma}
are smooth functions
in their arguments. Let
\Psi^{m+1}_{\gamma}=Z^{m+1}\varphi_{\gamma}
,
where
Z
stands for one of some chosen first order vector fields. It follows from (
1.24
)
and direct computation on the commutator
[\mu\Box_{g},Z^{m+1}]
that

\mu\Box_{g}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m+1}=\Phi_{\gamma}^{m+1}

(1.25)

with
\Phi_{\gamma}^{m+1}
containing the
(m+2)
-th order derivatives of
\varphi
. In fact,
both
\not{\nabla}Z^{m}\textrm{tr}\chi
and
\not{\nabla}^{2}Z^{m}\mu
appear in the expression of
\Phi_{\gamma}^{m+1}
,
where
\chi_{XX}=g(\mathscr{D}_{X}\mathring{L},X)
is the second fundamental form of
S_{s,u}

with
\mathscr{D}
being the Levi-Civita connection of
g
, and
\textrm{tr}\chi
is the trace of
\chi
on
S_{s,u}
.
Following the analogous procedures in Section 3-Section 11 of
[
11
]
or
in Section 2-Section 9 of
[
10
]
, we can derive the energy estimates for (
1.25
)
to obtain the appropriate smallness orders of
\delta
and time decay rates of
\Psi_{\gamma}^{m+1}
. However, in order
to look for the optimal exponent
\varepsilon_{k}^{*}
related to the
k-
th null condition, it is necessary to derive
the optimal smallness order of
\delta
and precise time decay rate for all related quantities.
This gives rise to certain essential difficulties.
Some of these key difficulties and our new strategies to overcome them are sketched as follows (which
are different from those in
[
11
]
):

\bullet
Control
\|Z^{m+1}\mu\|_{L^{2}}
precisely by the corresponding energies and fluxes so that the
highest
(m+1)-
order energies of
\varphi

admit the required optimal smallness orders of
\delta
and further the bootstrap assumptions can be closed.
In this process, note that
\|Z^{m+1}\mu\|_{L^{2}}

can be estimated from
\|Z^{m+1}\mathring{L}\mu\|_{L^{2}}
by integrating along integral curves of
\mathring{L}
.
In addition, due to the
k-
th null condition,
\mathring{L}\mu
can be composed of
\mathring{L}\phi

and
\not{d}\phi
with
\not{d}
being a differential on
S_{s,u}
. Thus,
in order to estimate
\|Z^{m+1}\mathring{L}\mu\|_{L^{2}}
, one needs to treat both
\|Z^{m+1}\mathring{L}\phi\|_{L^{2}}

and
\|\not{d}Z^{m+1}\phi\|_{L^{2}}
. Unfortunately, although it has been found that
\|Z^{m+1}\mathring{L}\phi\|_{L^{2}}

admits a better time decay rate than
\|\not{d}Z^{m+1}\phi\|_{L^{2}}
(see Lemma
3.3
), the optimal smallness order
of
\delta
cannot be obtained. Thus more careful analysis on the structure of
\mathring{L}\mu
is needed. A key observation here is that

\mathscr{A}=g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma a\gamma_{3}\cdots\gamma_{k}}\omega_{\alpha}\omega_{\beta}\omega_{\gamma}(m_{ab}\not{d}^{X}x^{b}\not{d}_{X}\phi)\omega_{\gamma_{3}}\cdots\omega_{\gamma_{k}}
is a good unknown
since
\|Z^{m+1}\mathscr{A}\|_{L^{2}}
admits the higher order smallness of
\delta
and better time decay rate than
\|\not{d}Z^{m+1}\phi\|_{L^{2}}
.
Note that
Z^{m+1}\mathring{L}\mu
consists of mainly
Z^{m+1}\mathscr{A}
and
Z^{m+1}\mathring{L}\phi
,
moreover,
\|Z^{m+1}\mathscr{A}\|_{L^{2}}
and
\|Z^{m+1}\mathring{L}\phi\|_{L^{2}}
are bounded by
\|R^{m}\not{\triangle}\mu\|_{L^{2}}
, where
R
is a specific vectorfield of
Z
which is projected by
\Omega
on
S_{s,u}
and
\not{\triangle}
is a Laplace operator of
S_{s,u}
.
Thus the remaining task is to treat
\|R^{m}\not{\triangle}\mu\|_{L^{2}}
.

\bullet
Deal with
\|R^{m}\not{\triangle}\mu\|_{L^{2}}
.
Note that
\Phi_{\gamma}^{m+1}
encompasses both
\not{d}Z^{m}\textrm{tr}\chi
and
\not{\nabla}^{2}Z^{m}\mu
. Consequently, as demonstrated in
[
10
,
11
]
, we can estimate the
L^{2}
norms of
\not{d}Z^{m}\textrm{tr}\chi
and
Z^{m}\not{\triangle}\mu
simultaneously, not solely
\|R^{m}\not{\triangle}\mu\|_{L^{2}}
.
By deriving the transport equations for
\not{\triangle}\mu
and
\textrm{tr}\chi
along
\mathring{L}
and making full use of the
k-
th null condition, the estimates of
\|\not{d}Z^{m}\textrm{tr}\chi\|_{L^{2}}

and
\|Z^{m}\not{\triangle}\mu\|_{L^{2}}
can be precisely controlled by the energies
E_{i,\leq m+2}
(
i=1,2
) and the fluxes
F_{1,\leq m+2}

(see (
3.102
)-(
3.103
) and (
4.287
)-(
4.288
)).
In the process, one of the main observations is that
\sum_{j=1}^{3}\not{d}\varphi_{j}\cdot\not{d}x^{j}
may be represented by
\mathring{L}\varphi
(see (
4.201
)). This leads to that the estimate on the basic terms
\|\not{d}Z^{m+1}\varphi_{j}\|_{L^{2}}
in

\|\not{d}Z^{m}\text{tr}\chi\|_{L^{2}}
can be bounded by
\int_{0}^{u}F_{1,m+2}(s,u^{\prime})du^{\prime}
(see (
5.302
)-(
5.303
)).
Then all required quantities are eventually estimated through the optimal smallness orders of
\delta

and better time decay rates, which are the keys to treat the case when
\varepsilon_{0}
is near
\varepsilon_{k}^{*}
and further to
close the arguments of bootstrap assumptions on
\varphi
.

On the other hand, in order to study the global Goursat problem for (
1.1
) and derive the a priori
uniform estimates of
\phi
in
B_{2\delta}
by energy methods,
the properties for
|\partial^{\alpha}L^{q}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{-1/2-q}
(
L=\partial_{t}+\partial_{r}
) on
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}

with the higher order smallness
O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}})
and the time decay rate of
t^{-1/2-q}

are crucial since the related boundary integrals on
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}
in the energy estimates require the
the smallness order of
O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}})
. However, all the estimates on
\phi
in
A_{2\delta}

are obtained under the modified frame
\{\mathring{L},T,R\}
,
where
\mathring{L}
,
T
,
R
are some suitable vectors approximating
L
,
-\partial_{r}=\frac{\underline{L}-L}{2}

(\underline{L}=\partial_{t}-\partial_{r})
,

\Omega=x^{1}\partial_{2}-x^{2}\partial_{1}
respectively on the time
t=1+2\delta
. To get the
L^{\infty}
estimates on
\phi
under the
derivatives
\{L,\underline{L},\Omega\}

and further obtain the required smallness and time decay rate of
L\phi
, we need to express the vector fields
\{L,\underline{L},\Omega\}

by
\{\mathring{L},T,R\}
. As in
[
10
]
, the most noteworthy expression is
L=c_{L}\mathring{L}+c_{T}T+c_{\Omega}\Omega
,
where
c_{L}
,
c_{T}
and
c_{\Omega}
are
smooth functions. One expects that the desired derivative
L\phi
and its derivatives can admit the smallness of higher
orders with respect to
\delta

and faster time decay rate. Unfortunately,
T\phi
and its derivatives have not enough smallness and time decay rate as needed,
it is hoped as in
[
10
]
that the coefficient
c_{T}
can make up for these deficiencies. The generality of equation (
1.1
) and
the optimal scope of
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,\varepsilon_{k}^{*})

make it more difficult to analyze the smallness orders and the time decay rates of the related coefficients
c_{T}
,
c_{\Omega}
and so on.
Thanks to the
k-
th null condition (
1.9
), we can find
their governing differential equations along
\mathring{L}
and subsequently
get the desired estimates of
c_{L}
,
c_{T}
and
c_{\Omega}
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, first, some preliminaries on
the related differential geometry and definitions
are given. Based on the studies of the local solution to (
1.1
), the crucial bootstrap assumptions
are given and further some important quantities are estimated in
A_{2\delta}
.
In Section
3
, we first carry out the energy estimates for the linearized equation
\mu\Box_{g}\Psi=\Phi

and define some suitable higher order weighted energies and fluxes as in
[
23
]
. Subsequently,
the higher order
L^{2}
estimates of some related quantities with the precise smallness orders of
\delta

and time decay rates are obtained.
In Section
4
, the top order
L^{2}
estimates on the derivatives of
\chi
and
\mu
are established.
On the other hand, the estimates for the error terms are derived in Section
5
.
In Section
6
and Section
7
, the global uniform estimates of the solution to (
1.1
) in
A_{2\delta}

and
B_{2\delta}
are obtained, respectively. Therefore, the proof of Theorem
1.1
can be completed
by the continuous induction argument.
In addition, some computations on the deformation tensor and commutator relations are given in Appendix A.
The proof on the local solution properties of (
1.1
) for
1\leq t\leq 1+2\delta
is given in Appendix B.

Through the whole paper, unless stated otherwise, Greek indices
\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\cdots\}
corresponding to the
time-space coordinates are chosen in
\{0,1,2\}
, Latin indices
\{i,j,a,b,c,\cdots\}
corresponding to the
spatial coordinates are
\{1,2\}
, and the Einstein summation convention to sum over repeated upper
and lower indices is used. In addition, the convention
f_{1}\lesssim f_{2}
means that there exists a generic positive constant

C
independent of the parameter
\delta>0
in (
1.2
) and the variables
(t,x)
such that
f_{1}\leq Cf_{2}
. The coefficients
g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)
are denoted as
g^{\alpha\beta}
by convenience.
Denote
g=g_{\alpha\beta}dx^{\alpha}dx^{\beta}
by a Lorentz metric, where
(g_{\alpha\beta})
is the inverse matrix of
(g^{\alpha\beta})
.

Finally, the following conventional notations will be used too:

\displaystyle t_{0}:=1+2\delta,

\displaystyle\omega^{i}:=\omega_{i}=\frac{x^{i}}{r},\ i=1,2,\quad\omega_{\perp}:=(-\omega^{2},\omega^{1}),

\displaystyle\partial_{x}=\nabla_{x}=(\partial_{1},\partial_{2},),\quad L:=\partial_{t}+\partial_{r},\quad\underline{L}:=\partial_{t}-\partial_{r},

\displaystyle\Omega:=\epsilon_{i}^{j}x^{i}\partial_{j},\quad S:=t\partial_{t}+r\partial_{r}=\frac{t-r}{2}\underline{L}+\frac{t+r}{2}L,

\displaystyle H_{i}:=t\partial_{i}+x^{i}\partial_{t}=\omega^{i}\big(\frac{r-t}{2}\underline{L}+\frac{t+r}{2}L\big)+\frac{t\omega^{i}_{\perp}}{r}\Omega,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t}:=\{(s,x):s=t,x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\},

\displaystyle\varphi=(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})=(\partial_{0}\phi,\partial_{1}\phi,\partial_{2}\phi),

where
\epsilon_{1}^{2}=1
,
\epsilon_{2}^{1}=-1
,
\epsilon_{i}^{i}=0
for
i=1,2
.

2
Preliminaries and some estimates under bootstrap assumptions

2.1
Geometry and basic equalities under null frames

We give some preliminaries on the
related geometry and definitions, which will be
utilized as basic tools to establish the a priori global estimates on the
smooth solution
\phi
to (
1.1
) in
A_{2\delta}
. This part is completely similar to
[
10
,
11
,
23
]
,
so we just list the notations and state the facts without going into the details.

The definitions of the optical function
u
, the inverse foliation density
\mu
and
the definition of
\vartheta
have been
given in (
1.19
), (
1.20
) and (
1.22
), respectively. Note that

\tilde{L}=-g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\partial_{\beta}

is a tangent vector field of the outgoing light cone
\{u=C\}
and
\tilde{L}t=\mu^{-1}
holds.

Set

\mathring{L}=\mu\tilde{L},\quad\mathring{\underline{L}}=\mu\mathring{L}+2T\quad\text{with $T=\mu\tilde{T}$
and $\tilde{T}=-g^{\alpha 0}\partial_{\alpha}-\mathring{L}$}.

Then

\mathring{L}
,
\mathring{\underline{L}}
and
X=\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta}

form a null frame with respect to the metric
g
.

In the new coordinate
(s,u,\vartheta)
of (
1.23
), the following subsets can be defined (see Figure
1
):

Definition 2.1
.

\begin{split}&\Sigma_{s}^{u}:=\{(s^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta):s^{\prime}=s,0\leq u^{\prime}\leq u\},\quad u\in[0,4\delta],\\
&C_{u}:=\{(s^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta):s^{\prime}\geq 1+2\delta,u^{\prime}=u\},\\
&C_{u}^{s}:=\{(s^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta):1+2\delta\leq s^{\prime}\leq s,u^{\prime}=u\},\\
&S_{s,u}:=\Sigma_{s}\cap C_{u},\\
&D^{s,u}:=\{(s^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta):1+2\delta\leq s^{\prime}<s,0\leq u^{\prime}\leq u\}.\end{split}

(2.26)

Figure 1:
The indications of some domains

The following geometric notations are needed.

Definition 2.2
.

For the metric
g
on the spacetime,

•

\underline{g}=(g_{ij})
is defined as the induced metric of
g
on
\Sigma_{t}
, i.e.,
\underline{g}(U,V)=g(U,V)
for any tangent vectors
U
and
V
of
\Sigma_{t}
;

•

\not{\Pi}_{\alpha}^{\beta}:=\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}-\delta_{\alpha}^{0}\mathring{L}^{\beta}+\mathring{L}_{\alpha}\tilde{T}^{\beta}
is the
projection tensor field on
S_{s,u}
of type
(1,1)
, where
\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}
is Kronecker delta;

•

define
\not{\xi}:=\not{\Pi}\xi
as the tensor field on
S_{s,u}
for the
(m,n)
-type spacetime tensor field

\xi
, whose components are

\not{\xi}^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}}_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n}}:=(\not{\Pi}\xi)^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}}_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n}}=\not{\Pi}_{\beta_{1}}^{\beta_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots\not{\Pi}_{\beta_{n}}^{\beta_{n}^{\prime}}\not{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\not{\Pi}_{\alpha_{m}^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{m}}\xi^{\alpha_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\alpha_{m}^{\prime}}_{\beta_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\beta_{n}^{\prime}}.

Specially,
\not{g}=(\not{g}_{\alpha\beta})
is the induced metric of
g
on
S_{s,u}
;

•

\not{g}^{XX}
is defined as the reciprocal of
\not{g}_{XX}
with
\not{g}_{XX}=g(X,X)
;

•

\mathscr{D}
and
\not{\nabla}
denote the Levi-Civita connection of
g
and
\not{g}
, respectively;

•

\Box_{g}:=g^{\alpha\beta}\mathscr{D}^{2}_{\alpha\beta}
,
\not{\triangle}:=\not{g}^{XX}\not{\nabla}^{2}_{X}
;

•

\mathcal{L}_{V}\xi
denotes the Lie derivative of
\xi
with respect to
V
and
\not{\mathcal{L}}_{V}\xi:=\not{\Pi}(\mathcal{L}_{V}\xi)
for any tensor field
\xi
and vector
V
;

•

when
\xi
is a
(m,n)
-type spacetime tensor field, the square of its norm is defined as

|\xi|^{2}:=g_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots g_{\alpha_{m}\alpha_{m}^{\prime}}g^{\beta_{1}\beta_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots g^{\beta_{n}\beta_{n}^{\prime}}\xi_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n}}^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}}\xi_{\beta_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\beta_{n}^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\alpha_{m}^{\prime}}.

In the null frame
\{\mathring{L},\mathring{\underline{L}},X\}
,

the second fundamental forms

\chi
and
\sigma
are defined as

\chi_{XX}:=g(\mathscr{D}_{X}\mathring{L},X),\quad\sigma_{XX}:=g(\mathscr{D}_{X}\tilde{T},X).

(2.27)

Meanwhile, define the
one-form tensors

\zeta
and
\eta
as

\zeta_{X}:=g(\mathscr{D}_{X}\mathring{L},\tilde{T}),\quad\eta_{X}:=-g(\mathscr{D}_{X}T,\mathring{L}).

(2.28)

For any vector field
V
, denote its associate
deformation tensor
by

{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(V)}\pi_{\alpha\beta}:=g(\mathscr{D}_{\alpha}V,\partial_{\beta})+g(\mathscr{D}_{\beta}V,\partial_{\alpha}).

(2.29)

For
t\geq t_{0}
, the following
error terms
are defined as

\begin{split}&\check{L}^{0}:=0,\ \check{L}^{i}:=\mathring{L}^{i}-\frac{x^{i}}{\varrho},\ \check{T}^{i}:=\tilde{T}^{i}+\frac{x^{i}}{\varrho},\ \check{\chi}_{XX}:=\chi_{XX}-\frac{1}{\varrho}\not{g}_{XX},\end{split}

(2.30)

here and below
\varrho=t-u
.
Note that in the new coordinate system
(s,u,\vartheta)
, it holds that

\mathring{L}=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}
and
T=\frac{\partial}{\partial u}-\Xi
with
\Xi=\Xi^{X}X
for some smooth function
\Xi^{X}
.
In addition,
[
23
, Lemma 3.66]
gives

Lemma 2.1
.

In domain
D^{s,u}
, the Jacobian determinant of the map
(s,u,\vartheta)\rightarrow(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2})
is

\det\frac{\partial(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2})}{\partial(s,u,\vartheta)}=\mu(\det\underline{g})^{-1/2}\sqrt{\not{g}_{XX}}.

(2.31)

On
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, the vector field
\Omega=x^{1}\partial_{2}-x^{2}\partial_{1}

is taken as the tangent derivative.
In order to project
\Omega
on
S_{s,u}
, as in (3.39b) of
[
23
]
, we define

R:=\not{\Pi}\Omega,\quad\not{d}:=\not{\Pi}d

to be the rotation vectorfield and the differential of
S_{s,u}
, respectively. Note that the explicit expression of
R
is

R=(\delta_{j}^{i}+g_{\alpha j}\mathring{L}^{\alpha}\tilde{T}^{i}){\Omega}^{j}\partial_{i}=\Omega-g_{aj}\tilde{T}^{a}{\Omega}^{j}\tilde{T}.

(2.32)

For brevity, one writes

\upsilon:=g_{ab}\tilde{T}^{a}{\Omega}^{b}=g_{aj}\check{T}^{a}\epsilon_{i}^{j}x^{i}-(g_{aj}-m_{aj})\frac{\epsilon_{i}^{j}x^{i}x^{a}}{\varrho}.

(2.33)

Then

R=\Omega-\upsilon\tilde{T}.

Definition 2.3
.

For any continuous function
f
and tensor field
\xi
, define

\displaystyle\int_{S_{s,u}}f:=\int_{S_{s,u}}fd\nu_{\not{g}}=\int_{\mathbb{S}}f(s,u,\vartheta)\sqrt{\not{g}_{XX}(s,u,\vartheta)}d\vartheta,

\displaystyle\int_{C^{s}_{u}}f:=\int_{t_{0}}^{s}\int_{S_{\tau,u}}f(\tau,u,\vartheta)d\nu_{\not{g}}d\tau,\quad\int_{\Sigma_{s}^{u}}f:=\int_{0}^{u}\int_{S_{s,u^{\prime}}}f(s,u^{\prime},\vartheta)d\nu_{\not{g}}du^{\prime},

\displaystyle\int_{D^{s,u}}f:=\int_{t_{0}}^{s}\int_{0}^{u}\int_{S_{\tau,u^{\prime}}}f(\tau,u^{\prime},\vartheta)d\nu_{\not{g}}du^{\prime}d\tau,\quad\|\xi\|_{s,u}:=\sqrt{\int_{\Sigma_{s}^{u}}|\xi|^{2}}.

Finally, for reader’s convenience, we recall the notations of contraction and trace as follows.

Definition 2.4
.

(1) If
\xi
is a
(0,2)
-type spacetime tensor,
\Lambda
is a 1-form,
U
and
V
are vector fields, then the contraction
of
\xi
with respect to
U
and
V
is defined as

\xi_{UV}:=\xi_{\alpha\beta}U^{\alpha}V^{\beta},

and the contraction of
\Lambda
with respect to
U
is

\Lambda_{U}:=\Lambda_{\alpha}U^{\alpha}.

(2) If
\xi
is a
(0,2)
-type tensor on
S_{s,u}
, the trace of
\xi
is defined as

\text{tr}\xi:=\not{g}^{XX}\xi_{XX}.

Note that
(g_{\alpha\beta})
is the inverse matrix of
(g^{\alpha\beta})
.
By
g^{\alpha\beta}g^{\lambda\kappa}g_{\kappa\beta}=g^{\alpha\lambda}
, it holds that

g^{\alpha\beta}g^{\lambda\kappa}(\partial_{\varphi_{\gamma}}g_{\kappa\beta})=-\partial_{\varphi_{\gamma}}g^{\alpha\lambda}.

(2.34)

With the help of
{\chi}
,
\sigma
,
{\zeta}
and
{\eta}

defined as before, one can derive some basic equalities in the frame
\{\mathring{L},\mathring{\underline{L}},X\}

or the frame
\{T,\mathring{\underline{L}},X\}
.

Let

\begin{split}&\displaystyle G_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}:=\partial_{\varphi_{\gamma}}g_{\alpha\beta}.\end{split}

(2.35)

For any vector fields
\displaystyle U=U^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}
and
\displaystyle V=V^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}
, set
G_{UV}^{\gamma}=G_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}U^{\alpha}V^{\beta}
. One has the following transport equation of
\mu
along
\mathring{L}
, whose proof is exact similar to
[
10
, Lemma 4.3]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

\mu
satisfies

\begin{split}\mathring{L}\mu=-\frac{1}{2}\mu G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}-\mu G_{\tilde{T}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}T\varphi_{\gamma}.\end{split}

(2.36)

Remark 2.1
.

In the analysis later, special attention is needed for the terms containing
T\varphi_{\gamma}
since
T\varphi_{\gamma}
has the worse
smallness and slower time decay rate than the ones of
\bar{Z}\varphi_{\gamma}
, where
\bar{Z}\in\{\mathring{L},R\}
.

As in
[
11
]
, the following explicit expressions hold:

\displaystyle\zeta_{X}=-\frac{1}{2}\Big\{G_{\tilde{T}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}+G_{\tilde{T}\tilde{T}}^{\gamma}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}-G_{X\tilde{T}}^{\gamma}{\mathring{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}+G^{\gamma}_{X\mathring{L}}{\tilde{T}}\varphi_{\gamma}\Big\},

(2.37)

\displaystyle\sigma_{XX}=-G_{X\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}-G_{X\tilde{T}}^{\gamma}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}G_{XX}^{\gamma}{\mathring{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}G_{XX}^{\gamma}{\tilde{T}}\varphi_{\gamma}-\chi_{XX}.

(2.38)

Note that the quantity “deformation tensor” defined in (
2.29
)
will occur in the forthcoming energy estimates. It is necessary to compute the components
of
{\vphantom{\pi}}{}^{(V)}\pi
in the null frame
\{\mathring{L},\underline{\mathring{L}},X\}
.
This will be given in Appendix A. In addition, as in
[
11
]
,
it follows from (
2.36
) and (
A.2
)-(
A.4
) that
the vector fields
\mathring{L}
and
T
appear frequently. Based on this, we need the equations for
\mathring{L}^{i}
and
\check{L}^{i}
under the actions of the derivatives of null frame
\{T,\mathring{L},X\}
as well as the connection coefficients of the frames.

Lemma 2.3
.

It holds that

\displaystyle\mathring{L}\mathring{L}^{i}=\frac{1}{2}G^{\gamma}_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}\tilde{T}^{i}-\big\{G^{\gamma}_{X\mathring{L}}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}-\frac{1}{2}G^{\gamma}_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}\big\}\not{d}^{X}x^{i},

(2.39)

\displaystyle\mathring{L}\big(\varrho\check{L}^{i}\big)=\varrho\mathring{L}\mathring{L}^{i},

(2.40)

\displaystyle\not{d}_{X}\mathring{L}^{i}=\textrm{tr}\chi{\not{d}_{X}}x^{i}-\{G^{\gamma}_{\mathring{L}\tilde{T}}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}G^{\gamma}_{\tilde{T}\tilde{T}}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}\}\tilde{T}^{i}-\frac{1}{2}G_{XX}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}\not{d}^{X}x^{i},

(2.41)

\displaystyle\not{d}_{X}\check{L}^{i}=\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}{\not{d}_{X}}x^{i}-\{G^{\gamma}_{\mathring{L}\tilde{T}}{\not{d}_{X}}\varphi_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}G^{\gamma}_{\tilde{T}\tilde{T}}{\not{d}_{X}}\varphi_{\gamma}\}\tilde{T}^{i}-\frac{1}{2}G_{XX}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}\not{d}^{X}x^{i},

(2.42)

\displaystyle T\mathring{L}^{i}=\{\not{d}_{X}\mu-\frac{1}{2}\mu G^{\gamma}_{\tilde{T}\tilde{T}}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}-G^{\gamma}_{X\mathring{L}}T\varphi_{\gamma}\}\not{d}^{X}x^{i}+\big\{\frac{1}{2}\mu G^{\gamma}_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\mu G^{\gamma}_{\tilde{T}\mathring{L}}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}\mu G^{\gamma}_{\tilde{T}\tilde{T}}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}\big\}\mathring{L}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}G^{\gamma}_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}T\varphi_{\gamma}\tilde{T}^{i}

(2.43)

and

\begin{split}&\mathscr{D}_{\mathring{L}}\mathring{L}=(\mu^{-1}\mathring{L}\mu)\mathring{L},\quad\mathscr{D}_{T}\mathring{L}=-\mathring{L}\mu\mathring{L}+\eta^{X}X,\quad\mathscr{D}_{X}\mathring{L}=-\zeta_{X}\mathring{L}+\textrm{tr}{\chi}X,\\
&\mathscr{D}_{\mathring{L}}T=-\mathring{L}\mu\mathring{L}-\mu\zeta^{X}X,\quad\mathscr{D}_{T}T=\mu\mathring{L}\mu\mathring{L}+(\mu^{-1}T\mu+\mathring{L}\mu)T-\mu(\not{d}^{X}\mu)X,\\
&\mathscr{D}_{X}T=\mu\zeta_{X}\mathring{L}+\mu^{-1}\eta_{X}T+\mu\textrm{tr}{\sigma}X,\\
&\mathscr{D}_{X}X=\not{\nabla}_{X}X+(\sigma_{XX}+\chi_{XX})\mathring{L}+\mu^{-1}\chi_{XX}T.\end{split}

(2.44)

In addition, the covariant derivatives of the frame
\{\mathring{\underline{L}},\mathring{L},X\}
are

\begin{split}&\mathscr{D}_{\mathring{\underline{L}}}{\mathring{L}}=-\mathring{L}\mu\mathring{L}+2\eta^{X}X,\quad\mathscr{D}_{\mathring{L}}\mathring{\underline{L}}=-2\mu\zeta^{X}X,\\
&\mathscr{D}_{\mathring{\underline{L}}}\mathring{\underline{L}}=(\mu^{-1}\mathring{\underline{L}}\mu+\mathring{L}\mu)\mathring{\underline{L}}-(2\mu\not{d}^{X}\mu)X.\end{split}

(2.45)

Proof.

The proofs are similar to these in Lemma 3.4-Lemma 3.6 in
[
11
]
so are omitted.
∎

As in
[
11
]
, to estimate
\varphi
, one needs to
derive the equations for
\varphi_{\gamma}
under the action of the covariant wave operator
\Box_{g}=g^{\alpha\beta}\mathscr{D}^{2}_{\alpha\beta}=g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}-g^{\alpha\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}
, where
\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
are Christoffel symbols with

\begin{split}\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\gamma\kappa}\big(G_{\kappa\beta}^{\nu}\partial_{\alpha}\varphi_{\nu}+G_{\alpha\kappa}^{\nu}\partial_{\beta}\varphi_{\nu}-G_{\alpha\beta}^{\nu}\partial_{\kappa}\varphi_{\nu}\big).\end{split}

Note that

\begin{split}\Box_{g}\varphi_{\gamma}=&\partial_{\varphi_{\nu}}g^{\alpha\beta}\big(-\partial_{\nu}\varphi_{\gamma}\partial_{\beta}\varphi_{\alpha}+\partial_{\nu}\varphi_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\varphi_{\gamma}\big)+\frac{1}{2}(g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\varphi_{\nu}}g_{\alpha\beta})g^{\lambda\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}\varphi_{\nu}\partial_{\lambda}\varphi_{\gamma}.\end{split}

(2.46)

In terms of

\displaystyle g^{\alpha\beta}=-\mathring{L}^{\alpha}\mathring{L}^{\beta}-\tilde{T}^{\alpha}\mathring{L}^{\beta}-\mathring{L}^{\alpha}\tilde{T}^{\beta}+(\not{d}_{X}x^{\alpha})(\not{d}^{X}x^{\beta}),

(2.47)

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}=\delta_{\alpha}^{0}\mathring{L}-\mu^{-1}\mathring{L}_{\alpha}T+g_{\alpha i}(\not{d}^{X}x^{i})X,

(2.48)

then in the frame
\{T,\mathring{L},X\}
, (
2.46
) can be rewritten as

\begin{split}\mu\Box_{g}\varphi_{\gamma}=&\frac{1}{2}(g^{\alpha\beta}G_{\alpha\beta}^{\nu})(\mu\not{d}^{X}\varphi_{\nu})\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}+f_{1}(\varphi,\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2})\varphi^{k-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathring{L}\varphi\\
(\not{d}_{X}x)\not{d}^{X}\varphi\\
\end{array}\right)T\varphi_{\gamma}\\
&+f_{2}(\varphi,\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2})\varphi^{k-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}T\varphi\\
\mu\mathring{L}\varphi\\
\mu(\not{d}_{X}x)\not{d}^{X}\varphi\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}\\
(\not{d}_{X}x)\not{d}^{X}\varphi_{\gamma}\\
\end{array}\right),\end{split}

(2.49)

where
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are the generic smooth functions with respect to their arguments, and
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}A_{1}\\
\vdots\\
A_{n}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}B_{1}\\
\vdots\\
B_{m}\\
\end{array}\right)
stands for the terms which conclude
A_{i}B_{j}

(1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq m)
.
It can be checked that in the right hand side of (
2.49
), the worst factor
T\varphi

is always accompanied by the “good” multipliers
such as
\mathring{L}\varphi
and
\not{d}\varphi
.

2.2
The bootstrap assumptions and some related estimates

As in
[
11
]
, the existence and properties of local in time solution to (
1.1
) with (
1.2
), (
1.14
) and
(
1.15
)-(
1.16
) can be established easily in the following Theorem,
whose proof is given in Appendix B.

Theorem 2.1
.

Under the assumptions (
1.14
) and
(
1.15
)-(
1.16
), when
\delta>0
is small, equation (
1.1
) with (
1.2
)
admits a local smooth solution
\phi\in C^{\infty}([1,t_{0}]\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Moreover,
for
m,n,p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}
, it holds that

(i)

\displaystyle|L^{m}\partial^{\kappa}\Omega^{p}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|\kappa|-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad r\in[1-2\delta,1+2\delta],

(2.50)

\displaystyle|\underline{L}^{n}\partial^{\kappa}\Omega^{p}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|\kappa|-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad r\in[1-3\delta,1+\delta].

(2.51)

(ii)

|\partial^{\kappa}\Omega^{p}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\delta^{3-|\kappa|-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad r\in[1-3\delta,1+\delta].

(2.52)

(iii)

|\underline{L}^{m}L^{n}\Omega^{p}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad r\in[1-2\delta,1+\delta].

(2.53)

It should be pointed out that the special null condition structures of 2D Chaplygin gases are extensively
applied in
[
11
]
and simplify many identities compared to the general null conditions in the paper.
Therefore, although the remainder of this subsection is analogous to Section 3 in
[
11
]
,
the differential equation of
\mu
and the last term on the right hand side of
(
2.36
) require to be analyzed more delicately.

To show the global existence of solution
\phi
to (
1.1
) with (
1.2
) near
C_{0}
,
we will utilize the bootstrap argument and make the following bootstrap assumptions in
D^{s,u}
:

\begin{split}&\delta^{l}\|\mathring{L}Z^{\jmath}\varphi_{\gamma}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{s}^{u})}+\delta^{l}\|\not{d}Z^{\jmath}\varphi_{\gamma}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{s}^{u})}\leq M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}s^{-3/2},\\
&\delta^{l}\|\mathring{\underline{L}}Z^{\jmath}\varphi_{\gamma}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{s}^{u})}+\delta^{l-1}\|Z^{\jmath}\varphi_{\gamma}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{s}^{u})}\leq M\delta^{-\varepsilon_{0}}s^{-1/2},\\
&\delta\|\not{\nabla}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{s}^{u})}+\|\not{\nabla}^{2}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{s}^{u})}\leq M\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}s^{-5/2},\\
&\delta^{l}\|\mathring{L}Z^{\jmath}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{s}^{u})}+\delta^{l}\|\not{d}Z^{\jmath}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{s}^{u})}\leq M\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}s^{-3/2},\\
&\delta^{l}\|Z^{\jmath}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{s}^{u})}\leq M\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}s^{-1/2},\\
\end{split}

(
\star
)

where
|\jmath|\leq N
,
N
is a fixed large positive integer,
M
is some positive constant to be suitably chosen
(at least double bounds of the corresponding quantities on time
t_{0}
in Theorem
2.1
),

Z\in\{\varrho\mathring{L},T,R\}
, and
l
is the number of
T
included in
Z^{\jmath}
.

One can now derive a rough estimate of
\mu
under the assumptions (
\star
) as follows. By

1=g_{ij}\tilde{T}^{i}\tilde{T}^{j}=\big(1+O(M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-k/2})\big)\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}|\tilde{T}^{i}|^{2},

then

|\tilde{T}^{i}|,|\mathring{L}^{i}|\leq 1+O(M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-k/2}).

(2.54)

Moreover, by
|\not{d}x^{i}|^{2}=\not{g}^{ab}\not{d}_{a}x^{i}\not{d}_{b}x^{i}=g^{ii}+(g^{0i})^{2}-(\tilde{T}^{i})^{2}
,
it follows from
(\star)
and (
2.54
) that

|\not{d}x^{i}|\lesssim 1.

(2.55)

In addition,
|\mathring{L}(\varrho\check{L}^{i})|\lesssim M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-k/2}
holds
by (
2.40
) and
(\star)
, which further gives that through integrating
\mathring{L}(\varrho\check{L}^{i})

along integral curves of
\mathring{L}
and
using
\check{T}^{i}=-g^{0i}-\check{L}^{i}
,

|\check{L}^{i}|+|\check{T}^{i}|\lesssim M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-1}\ln s.

(2.56)

Due to
\displaystyle g_{ij}(\check{T}^{i}-\frac{x^{i}}{\varrho})(\check{T}^{j}-\frac{x^{j}}{\varrho})=1
, then

(g_{ij}\omega^{i}\omega^{j})\frac{r^{2}}{\varrho^{2}}-(2g_{ij}\check{T}^{i}\omega^{j})\frac{r}{\varrho}+g_{ij}\check{T}^{i}\check{T}^{j}-1=0.

Thus

\check{\varrho}:=\frac{r}{\varrho}-1=\frac{1-g_{ij}\omega^{i}\omega^{j}-g_{ij}\check{T}^{i}\check{T}^{j}+2g_{ij}\check{T}^{i}\omega^{j}}{\sqrt{g_{ij}\omega^{i}\omega^{j}-(g_{ij}\omega^{i}\omega^{j})(g_{ab}\check{T}^{a}\check{T}^{b})+(g_{ij}\check{T}^{i}\omega^{j})^{2}}+g_{ij}\omega^{i}\omega^{j}-g_{ij}\check{T}^{i}\omega^{j}}.

(2.57)

This yields

|\check{\varrho}|\lesssim M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-1}\ln s,

(2.58)

and hence by the definition of
\upsilon
in (
2.33
),

|\upsilon|\lesssim M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}\ln s.

(2.59)

To estimate
\mu
, one needs to handle the last term in (
2.36
).
It follows from (
2.48
) and (
2.34
) that

\begin{split}G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}T\varphi_{\gamma}=&G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{0}T^{i}(\mathring{L}\varphi_{i})-G^{\gamma}_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}\mathring{L}_{\gamma}\tilde{T}^{i}T\varphi_{i}+\mu g_{\gamma i}G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}(\not{d}^{X}x^{i})\tilde{T}^{j}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{j}\\
=&G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{0}T^{i}(\mathring{L}\varphi_{i})+\underline{(\partial_{\varphi_{\gamma}}g^{\alpha\beta})\mathring{L}_{\alpha}\mathring{L}_{\beta}\mathring{L}_{\gamma}}\tilde{T}^{i}T\varphi_{i}+\mu g_{\gamma i}G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}(\not{d}^{X}x^{i})\tilde{T}^{j}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{j}.\end{split}

(2.60)

The null condition (
1.9
) means that
the underline factor in (
2.60
) can be written as

\begin{split}&-G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}_{\gamma}=(\partial_{\varphi_{\gamma}}g^{\alpha\beta})\mathring{L}_{\alpha}\mathring{L}_{\beta}\mathring{L}_{\gamma}=kg^{\alpha\beta,\gamma\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}}\varphi_{\gamma_{2}}\cdots\varphi_{\gamma_{k}}\mathring{L}_{\alpha}\mathring{L}_{\beta}\mathring{L}_{\gamma}+(\partial_{\varphi_{\gamma}}h^{\alpha\beta})\mathring{L}_{\alpha}\mathring{L}_{\beta}\mathring{L}_{\gamma}\\
=&kg^{\alpha\beta,\gamma\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}}\varphi_{\gamma_{2}}\cdots\varphi_{\gamma_{k}}m_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}m_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}m_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}\frac{\tilde{x}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}{\varrho}\frac{\tilde{x}^{\beta^{\prime}}}{\varrho}\frac{\tilde{x}^{\gamma^{\prime}}}{\varrho}+f(\varphi,\check{L}^{1},\check{L}^{2},\frac{x}{\varrho})\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\varphi^{k}\\
\check{L}^{1}\varphi^{k-1}\\
\check{L}^{2}\varphi^{k-1}\end{array}\right)\\
=&kg^{\alpha\beta,\gamma\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}}\varphi_{\gamma_{2}}\cdots\varphi_{\gamma_{k}}\omega_{\alpha}\omega_{\beta}\omega_{\gamma}+f(\varphi,\check{L}^{1},\check{L}^{2},\frac{x}{\varrho},\check{\varrho})\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\varphi^{k}\\
\check{L}^{1}\varphi^{k-1}\\
\check{L}^{2}\varphi^{k-1}\\
\check{\varrho}\varphi^{k-1}\end{array}\right)\\
=&f(\varphi,\check{L}^{1},\check{L}^{2},\frac{x}{\varrho},\check{\varrho})\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\varphi^{k}\\
\check{L}^{1}\varphi^{k-1}\\
\check{L}^{2}\varphi^{k-1}\\
\check{\varrho}\varphi^{k-1}\\
(\mathring{L}\phi)\varphi^{k-2}\\
(\not{d}^{X}x)(\not{d}_{X}\phi)\varphi^{k-2}\end{array}\right),\end{split}

(2.61)

where
\frac{x^{i}}{\varrho}=\check{\varrho}\omega_{i}+\omega_{i}
,
\varphi_{\gamma_{j}}=\delta_{\gamma_{j}}^{0}\mathring{L}\phi-\mathring{L}_{\gamma_{j}}\tilde{T}^{a}\varphi_{a}+g_{\gamma_{j}a}\not{d}^{X}x^{a}\not{d}_{X}\phi
, (
2.48
) and (
1.9
) are used,

(\tilde{x}^{0},\tilde{x}^{1},\tilde{x}^{2})=(\varrho,x^{1},x^{2})

and
(\omega_{0},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=(-1,\omega^{1},\omega^{2})
. Therefore,

|(\partial_{\varphi_{\gamma}}g^{\alpha\beta})\mathring{L}_{\alpha}\mathring{L}_{\beta}\mathring{L}_{\gamma}|\lesssim M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-k/2}

holds by (
2.54
)-(
2.56
), (
2.58
) and
(\star)
.
Subsequently, it follows from the expression (
2.60
) that

|G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}T\varphi_{\gamma}|\lesssim\mu M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-(k+2)/2}+M^{k+1}\delta^{k-(k+1)\varepsilon_{0}}s^{-(k+1)/2}.

(2.62)

Together with
(\star)
and (
2.36
), it is easy to
obtain
|\mathring{L}\mu|\lesssim M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-(k+2)/2}\mu+M^{k+1}\delta^{k-(k+1)\varepsilon_{0}}s^{-(k+1)/2}
. For
\delta>0
suitably small, by
integrating
\mathring{L}\mu
along integral curves of
\mathring{L}
and noting
\displaystyle\mu=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(g^{0i}\omega_{i})^{2}+g^{ij}\omega_{i}\omega_{j}}}

=1+O(\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})})
on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
,
one directly has that due to
\varepsilon_{0}<\varepsilon_{k}^{*}
,

\mu=1+O(M^{k+1}\delta^{k-(k+1)\varepsilon_{0}})

(2.63)

which has a positive lower bound as long as
(\star)
holds and
\delta
is small enough.

To improve the assumptions (
\star
), one can rewrite equation (
2.49
) in the new frame

\{\mathring{\underline{L}},\mathring{L},X\}
as

\mathring{L}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2\varrho}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}=\mu\not{\triangle}\varphi_{\gamma}+H_{\gamma},

(2.64)

here one has used the fact
\mu\Box_{g}\varphi_{\gamma}=-\mathscr{D}_{\mathring{L}\mathring{\underline{L}}}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma}+\mu\not{g}^{XX}\mathscr{D}_{X}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma}=-\mathring{L}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}-2\mu\zeta^{X}(\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma})+\mu\not{\triangle}\varphi_{\gamma}-\mu(\textrm{tr}\sigma+\textrm{tr}\chi)\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}-\textrm{tr}\chi T\varphi_{\gamma}

by (
2.44
) and (
2.45
). In addition, by (
2.37
)-(
2.38
),

\begin{split}H_{\gamma}=&-(\textrm{tr}\check{\chi})T\varphi_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2\varrho}\mu\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}+f_{1}(\varphi,\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2})\varphi^{k-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathring{L}\varphi\\
(\not{d}_{X}x)\not{d}^{X}\varphi\\
\end{array}\right)T\varphi_{\gamma}\\
&+f_{2}(\varphi,\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2},\not{d}x,\not{g})\varphi^{k-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}T\varphi\\
\mu\mathring{L}\varphi\\
\mu\not{d}\varphi\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}\\
\not{d}\varphi_{\gamma}\\
\end{array}\right).\end{split}

(2.65)

As in
[
10
,
11
]
, it is noted from the expression of
H_{\gamma}
that if there are the terms including the factor
T\varphi_{\alpha}

which admits the “bad” smallness or slow time decay rate,
then there always appear another product factor equipped with the “good”
smallness and fast time decay rate, e.g.

\textrm{tr}\check{\chi},\mathring{L}\varphi
and so on.

Unless stated otherwise, from now on, in this subsection, the pointwise estimates for the related quantities
are all carried out inside domain
D^{s,u}
.

It follows from the expression (
2.64
) that the elaborate
estimate of
\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}
can be achieved by integrating (
2.64
)
along integral curves of
\mathring{L}
. To this end, one should estimate
\check{\chi}
and
other terms in
H_{\gamma}
.

To estimate
\check{\chi}
, one needs the structure equation for
\chi
.
Analogously to
[
10
, Lemma 5.2]
, one has

Lemma 2.4
.

The second fundamental form
\chi
and its “error” form
\check{\chi}
, defined in (
2.27
) and (
2.30
)
respectively, satisfy the following structure equations:

\begin{split}\mathring{L}\chi_{XX}=&-G_{X\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}(\not{d}_{X}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma})+\frac{1}{2}G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\not{\nabla}_{X}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}G_{XX}^{\gamma}(\mathring{L}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma})-\big\{\frac{1}{2}G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}+G_{\tilde{T}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}\chi_{XX}\\
&+(G_{X\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma})\textrm{tr}\chi+(\textrm{tr}\chi)\chi_{XX}+f(\varphi,\not{d}x,\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2})\varphi^{k-2}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathring{L}\varphi\\
\not{d}\varphi\\
\end{array}\right)^{2},\end{split}

(2.66)

\begin{split}\not{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\chi_{XX}=&\not{\nabla}_{X}^{2}\mu-\frac{1}{2}\big\{\mu G_{\tilde{T}\tilde{T}}^{\gamma}\not{\nabla}_{X}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma}+2G_{X\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\not{d}_{X}T\varphi_{\gamma}-G_{XX}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}T\varphi_{\gamma}\big\}-\mu(\textrm{tr}\chi)^{2}\not{g}_{XX}\\
&-\{G_{\tilde{T}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}+G_{\tilde{T}\tilde{T}}^{\gamma}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}-G_{X\tilde{T}}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}\}d_{X}\mu+\Upsilon\cdot\chi_{XX}\\
&+f_{1}(\varphi,\not{d}x,\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2})\varphi^{k-2}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\varphi\not{d}\mathring{L}^{1}\\
\varphi\not{d}\mathring{L}^{2}\\
\mathring{L}\varphi\\
\not{d}\varphi\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mu\mathring{L}\varphi\\
\mu\not{d}\varphi\\
T{\varphi}\\
\end{array}\right)+f_{2}(\varphi,\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2})\varphi^{k-1}\not{\nabla}^{2}x\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mu\mathring{L}\varphi\\
T{\varphi}\\
\end{array}\right).\end{split}

(2.67)

And hence,

\begin{split}\mathring{L}\check{\chi}_{XX}=&-G_{X\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}(\not{d}_{X}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma})+\frac{1}{2}G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\not{\nabla}_{X}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}G_{XX}^{\gamma}(\mathring{L}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma})-\big\{\frac{1}{2}G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}+G_{\tilde{T}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}\check{\chi}_{XX}\\
&-\frac{\not{g}_{XX}}{\varrho}\big\{\frac{1}{2}G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}+G_{\tilde{T}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}+(G_{X\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma})\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}+\frac{1}{\varrho}(G_{X\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma})+(\textrm{tr}\check{\chi})\check{\chi}_{XX}\\
&+f(\varphi,\not{d}x,\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2})\varphi^{k-2}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathring{L}\varphi\\
\not{d}\varphi\\
\end{array}\right)^{2},\end{split}

(2.68)

\begin{split}\not{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\check{\chi}_{XX}=&\not{\nabla}_{X}^{2}\mu-\frac{1}{2}\big\{\mu G_{\tilde{T}\tilde{T}}^{\gamma}\not{\nabla}_{X}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma}+2G_{X\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\not{d}_{X}T\varphi_{\gamma}-G_{XX}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}T\varphi_{\gamma}\big\}-\mu(\textrm{tr}\check{\chi})^{2}\not{g}_{XX}\\
&-\{G_{\tilde{T}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}+G_{\tilde{T}\tilde{T}}^{\gamma}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}-G_{X\tilde{T}}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}\}d_{X}\mu+\Upsilon\cdot\check{\chi}_{XX}+\frac{1}{2\varrho}\Theta\cdot\not{g}_{XX}\\
&+\frac{\mu-1}{\varrho^{2}}\not{g}_{XX}+f_{1}(\varphi,\not{d}x,\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2})\varphi^{k-2}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\varphi\not{d}\mathring{L}^{1}\\
\varphi\not{d}\mathring{L}^{2}\\
\mathring{L}\varphi\\
\not{d}\varphi\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mu\mathring{L}\varphi\\
\mu\not{d}\varphi\\
T{\varphi}\\
\end{array}\right)\\
&+f_{2}(\varphi,\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2})\varphi^{k-1}\not{\nabla}^{2}x\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mu\mathring{L}\varphi\\
T{\varphi}\\
\end{array}\right),\end{split}

(2.69)

where

\displaystyle\Upsilon=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mu G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}+\mu G_{\tilde{T}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}-\frac{1}{2}G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}T\varphi_{\gamma}-\frac{3}{2}\mu G_{X\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\not{d}^{X}\varphi_{\gamma}-\frac{1}{2}\mu G_{X\tilde{T}}^{\gamma}\not{d}^{X}\varphi_{\gamma}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\not{g}^{XX}G_{XX}^{\gamma}T\varphi_{\gamma}-\frac{1}{2}G_{\mathring{L}\tilde{T}}^{\gamma}T\varphi_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}\mu\not{g}^{XX}G_{XX}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma},

(2.70)

\displaystyle\Theta=

\displaystyle\mu G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}+2\mu G_{\tilde{T}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}-G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}T\varphi_{\gamma}+\mu G_{X\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}\not{d}^{X}\varphi_{\gamma}+3G_{X\tilde{T}}^{\gamma}\not{d}^{X}\varphi_{\gamma}

\displaystyle-\mu\not{g}^{XX}G_{XX}^{\gamma}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}-\not{g}^{XX}G_{XX}^{\gamma}T\varphi_{\gamma}-G_{\mathring{L}\tilde{T}}^{\gamma}T\varphi_{\gamma},

(2.71)

and
f,f_{1},f_{2}
are the generic smooth functions of their arguments.

Based on (
2.68
),
\check{\chi}
can be estimated by integrating along integral curves of
\mathring{L}
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Under the assumptions
(\star)
, when
\delta>0
is small, it holds that

|\check{\chi}|=|\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}|\lesssim M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-2}\ln s.

(2.72)

Meanwhile,

|\chi|=\frac{1}{\varrho}+O(M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-2}\ln s).

(2.73)

Proof.

By
\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}=\not{g}^{XX}\check{\chi}_{XX}
, then

\mathring{L}\big(\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}\big)=-2(\textrm{tr}\check{\chi})^{2}-\frac{2}{\varrho}\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}+\not{g}^{XX}\mathring{L}\check{\chi}_{XX}.

(2.74)

Substituting (
2.68
) into (
2.74
), and using assumptions
(\star)
, (
2.54
) and (
2.55
) to estimate
the right hand side of (
2.74
) except
\check{\chi}
itself, one can get

|\mathring{L}\big(\varrho^{2}\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}\big)|\lesssim M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-k/2}+M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-(k+2)/2}|\varrho^{2}\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}|+\varrho^{-2}|\varrho^{2}\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}|^{2}.

Thus, for small
\delta>0
, one has

|\check{\chi}|\lesssim M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-2}\ln s.

∎

Additionally, it is noted that the operator
R
is just only the scaling operator
r\not{\nabla}
under
the assumptions
(\star)
and the estimate (
2.72
), which are similar to the ones in
[
23
, Lemma 12.22]
.

Corollary 2.1
.

Under the assumptions
(\star)
, when
\delta>0
is small,

1.

if
\xi
is a 1-form on
S_{s,u}
, then

\displaystyle(\xi_{a}R^{a})^{2}\sim r^{2}|\xi|^{2},

(2.75)

\displaystyle|\not{\mathcal{L}}_{R}\xi|^{2}\sim r^{2}|\not{\nabla}\xi|^{2}+O(M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-1}\ln s)|\xi|^{2};

(2.76)

2.

if
\xi
is a 2-form on
S_{s,u}
, then

\begin{split}|\not{\mathcal{L}}_{R}\xi|^{2}\sim r^{2}|\not{\nabla}\xi|^{2}+O(M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})}s^{-1}\ln s)|\xi|^{2}\end{split}

(2.77)

and

|\not{\nabla}^{2}\xi|\lesssim\varrho^{-2}|\not{\mathcal{L}}_{R}^{\leq 2}\xi|.

(2.78)

To close the bootstrap
assumptions
(\star)
, one can improve the estimate of
\varphi
by (
2.64
) to obtain the following proposition,
whose proofs are same as those in
[
10
, Proposition 6.3 and Corollary 6.3]
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Under the assumptions
(\star)
, for any operate
Z\in\{\varrho\mathring{L},T,R\}
, when
\delta>0
is small,
it holds that for
m\leq N-1
,

\begin{split}&|\not{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}^{m;l,p}\check{\chi}|\lesssim M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})-l}s^{-2}\ln s,\quad|\not{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}^{m+1;l,p}\not{d}x^{j}|\lesssim\delta^{-l},\quad\\
&|Z^{m+1;l,p}\check{L}^{j}|+|\not{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}^{m;l,p}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(R)}\not{\pi}|+|\not{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}^{m;l,p}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(R)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}}|+|Z^{m+1;l,p}\check{\varrho}|\lesssim M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})-l}s^{-1}\ln s,\\
&|\not{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}^{m;l,p}R|+|Z^{m+1;l,p}\upsilon|\lesssim M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})-l}\ln s,\quad|Z^{m+1;l,p}\mu|\lesssim M^{k+1}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})-\varepsilon_{0}-l},\\
&|\not{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}^{m;l,p}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(T)}\not{\pi}|\lesssim M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})-1-l}s^{-1}+s^{-l-1},\quad|\not{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}^{m;l,p}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(T)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}}|\lesssim M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})-1-l}s^{-1},\\
&|\not{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}^{m;l,p}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(R)}\not{\pi}_{T}|\lesssim(M^{k}\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})-l}+M^{2k}\delta^{2k(1-\varepsilon_{0})-1-l})s^{-1}\ln s,\end{split}

(2.79)

and

\begin{split}&\delta|Z^{m;l,p}\varphi_{\gamma}(s,u,\vartheta)|+|Z^{m;l,p}\phi(s,u,\vartheta)|\lesssim\delta^{2-l-\varepsilon_{0}}s^{-1/2},\end{split}

(2.80)

where
(m;l,p)
means the numbers of
Z
,
T
and
\varrho\mathring{L}
are
m
,
l
and
p
respectively.

Noted that as stated in the end of
[
11
, Section 5]
, all the related constants
in Proposition
2.2
can be independent of
M
. Therefore,
from now on, we may apply these estimates independent of
M
.

3
Energy estimates and some higher order
L^{2}
estimates

As in
[
10
]
, to close the bootstrap assumptions
(\star)
, one needs further refined estimates than those derived in Subsection
2.2
. Note that
\varphi_{\gamma}
satisfies the nonlinear equation (
2.49
), and each
derivative of
\varphi_{\gamma}
also fulfills similar equation with the same metric. Thus, one may focus on
the energy estimates for any smooth
function
\Psi
solving the following linear equation

\mu\Box_{g}\Psi=\Phi,

(3.81)

for a given function
\Phi
, where
\Psi
and its derivatives vanish on
C_{0}^{s}
.

As in
[
10
]
, we choose two multipliers
V_{1}\Psi:=\varrho^{2\iota}\mathring{L}\Psi

(0<\iota<\frac{1}{2})

and
V_{2}\Psi:=\mathring{\underline{L}}\Psi
, the energies
E_{i}[\Psi](s,u)
and fluxes
F_{i}[\Psi](s,u)

are defined after performing integration by part to
\mu(\Box_{g}\Psi)(V_{i}\Psi)
over
D^{s,u}

(i=1,2)
,

\displaystyle E_{1}[\Psi](s,u):=\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{s}^{u}}\mu\varrho^{2\iota}\{(\mathring{L}\Psi)^{2}+|\not{d}\Psi|^{2}\},

(3.82)

\displaystyle E_{2}[\Psi](s,u):=\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{s}^{u}}\{(\mathring{\underline{L}}\Psi)^{2}+\mu^{2}|\not{d}\Psi|^{2}\},

(3.83)

\displaystyle F_{1}[\Psi](s,u):=\displaystyle\int_{C_{u}^{s}}\varrho^{2\iota}(\mathring{L}\Psi)^{2},

(3.84)

\displaystyle F_{2}[\Psi](s,u):=\displaystyle\int_{C_{u}^{s}}\mu|\not{d}\Psi|^{2}.

(3.85)

Thus,

E_{i}[\Psi](s,u)-E_{i}[\Psi](t_{0},u)+F_{i}[\Psi](s,u)=-\displaystyle\int_{D^{s,u}}\Phi\cdot V_{i}\Psi-\int_{D^{s,u}}\frac{1}{2}\mu Q_{\alpha\beta}[\Psi]{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(V_{i})}\pi^{\alpha\beta},

(3.86)

where

Q_{\alpha\beta}[\Psi]:=(\partial_{\alpha}\Psi)(\partial_{\beta}\Psi)-\frac{1}{2}g_{\alpha\beta}g^{\nu\lambda}(\partial_{\nu}\Psi)(\partial_{\lambda}\Psi)

and
{\vphantom{\pi}}{}^{(V_{i})}\pi
is the deformation tensor with respect to the vector field
V_{i}
as defined in (
2.29
). Since
\frac{1}{2}\mu Q_{\alpha\beta}[\Psi]{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(V_{i})}\pi^{\alpha\beta}

(i=1,2)
have the same expressions as (7.7) and (7.8) in
[
10
]
,
one then can derive that

\begin{split}&\int_{D^{s,u}}-\frac{1}{2}\mu Q^{\alpha\beta}[\Psi]{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(V_{1})}\pi_{\alpha\beta}\\
\lesssim&\delta^{k(1-\varepsilon_{0})-\varepsilon_{0}}\int_{t_{0}}^{s}\tau^{-3/2}E_{1}[\Psi](\tau,u)d\tau+\delta^{-1}\int_{0}^{u}F_{1}[\Psi](s,u^{\prime})du^{\prime}+\delta\int_{t_{0}}^{s}\tau^{2\iota-2}E_{2}[\Psi](\tau,u)d\tau\end{split}

(3.87)

and

\begin{split}&\int_{D^{s,u}}-\frac{1}{2}\mu Q^{\alpha\beta}[\Psi]{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(V_{2})}\pi_{\alpha\beta}\\
\lesssim&\delta^{-1}\int_{0}^{u}F_{2}[\Psi](s,u^{\prime})du^{\prime}+\delta^{-1}\int_{0}^{u}F_{1}[\Psi](s,u^{\prime})du^{\prime}+\int_{t_{0}}^{s}\tau^{-2}E_{2}[\Psi](\tau,u)d\tau.\end{split}

(3.88)

And hence,

\begin{split}&\delta E_{2}[\Psi](s,u)+\delta F_{2}[\Psi](s,u)+E_{1}[\Psi](s,u)+F_{1}[\Psi](s,u)\\
\lesssim&\delta E_{2}[\Psi](t_{0},u)+E_{1}[\Psi](t_{0},u)+\delta\int_{D^{s,u}}|\Phi\cdot\mathring{\underline{L}}\Psi|+\int_{D^{s,u}}\varrho^{2\iota}|\Phi\cdot\mathring{L}\Psi|.\end{split}

(3.89)

Choose
\Psi=\Psi_{\gamma}^{m+1}=Z^{m+1}\varphi_{\gamma}
and then

\Phi=\Phi_{\gamma}^{m+1}=\mu\Box_{g}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m+1}
(
m\leq 2N-6
)
so that (
3.81
) holds. Note that

\begin{split}\Phi_{\gamma}^{m+1}&=\mu[\Box_{g},Z]\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}+Z\big(\mu\Box_{g}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}\big)-(Z\mu)\Box_{g}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}\\
&=\mu\mathscr{D}^{\alpha}({{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z)}C_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,\alpha})+(Z+{\vphantom{\lambda}}^{(Z)}\lambda)\Phi_{\gamma}^{m},\end{split}

(3.90)

where

\begin{split}&{{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z)}C_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,\alpha}=\big({\vphantom{\pi}}^{(Z)}\pi_{\nu\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\nu\alpha}(g_{\kappa\lambda}{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(Z)}\pi^{\kappa\lambda})\big)g^{\nu\beta}\partial_{\beta}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m},\\
&{\vphantom{\lambda}}{}^{(Z)}\lambda=-\mu^{-1}{\vphantom{\pi}}{}^{(Z)}\pi_{\mathring{L}T}+\frac{1}{2}\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}-\mu^{-1}Z\mu,\\
&\Psi_{\gamma}^{0}=\varphi_{\gamma},\quad\Phi_{\gamma}^{0}=\mu\Box_{g}\varphi_{\gamma},\end{split}

(3.91)

and
\Phi_{\gamma}^{0}
equals
the right hand side of (
2.49
).
Consequently, for
\Psi_{\gamma}^{m+1}=Z_{m+1}Z_{m}\cdots Z_{1}\varphi_{\gamma}
with
Z_{j}\in\{\varrho\mathring{L},T,R\}
,
then by (
3.90
), the induction argument gives

\begin{split}\Phi_{\gamma}^{m+1}=&\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big(Z_{m+1}+{\vphantom{\lambda}}^{(Z_{m+1})}\lambda\big)\dots\big(Z_{m+2-j}+{\vphantom{\lambda}}^{(Z_{m+2-j})}\lambda\big)\big(\mu\mathscr{D}^{\alpha}{{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z_{m+1-j})}C_{\gamma}^{m-j}}_{,\alpha}\big)\\
&+\mu\mathscr{D}^{\alpha}{{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z_{m+1})}C_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,\alpha}+\big(Z_{m+1}+{\vphantom{\lambda}}^{(Z_{m+1})}\lambda\big)\dots\big(Z_{1}+{\vphantom{\lambda}}^{(Z_{1})}\lambda\big)\Phi_{\gamma}^{0}\\
&=:J_{1}^{m+1}+J_{2}^{m+1},\qquad m\geq 1,\\
\Phi_{\gamma}^{1}=&\big(Z_{1}+{\vphantom{\lambda}}^{(Z_{1})}\lambda\big)\Phi_{\gamma}^{0}+\mu\mathscr{D}^{\alpha}{{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z_{1})}C_{\gamma}^{0}}_{,\alpha},\end{split}

(3.92)

where
J_{1}^{m+1}
and
J_{2}^{m+1}
stand for the first and the second line in the right hand side of (
3.92
), respectively.

By (
A.2
)-(
A.4
) in Appendix A, one has

\begin{split}&{\vphantom{\lambda}}{}^{(T)}\lambda=\frac{1}{2}\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(T)}\not{\pi},\quad{\vphantom{\lambda}}^{(\varrho\mathring{L})}\lambda=\varrho\textrm{tr}_{\not{g}}\check{\chi}+2,\quad{\vphantom{\lambda}}^{(R)}\lambda=\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(R)}\not{\pi}.\end{split}

(3.93)

In addition, notice that in the null frame
\{\mathring{\underline{L}},\mathring{L},X\}
, the
term
\mu\mathscr{D}^{\alpha}{{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z)}C_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,\alpha}
can be written as

\begin{split}\mu\mathscr{D}^{\alpha}{{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z)}C_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,\alpha}=&-\frac{1}{2}\mathring{L}\big({{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z)}C_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,\mathring{\underline{L}}}\big)-\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\underline{L}}\big({{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z)}C_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,\mathring{L}}\big)+\not{\nabla}^{X}\big(\mu{{\vphantom{\not{C}}}{}^{(Z)}\not{C}_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,X}\big)\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\big(\mathring{L}\mu+\mu\textrm{tr}{\chi}+\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(T)}\not{\pi}\big){{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z)}C_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,\mathring{L}}-\frac{1}{2}\textrm{tr}\chi{{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z)}C_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,\mathring{\underline{L}}},\end{split}

(3.94)

where

\begin{split}&{{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z)}C_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,\mathring{L}}={\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}X}(\not{d}^{X}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m})-\frac{1}{2}(\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi})\mathring{L}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m},\\
&{{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z)}C_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,\mathring{\underline{L}}}=-2({\vphantom{\pi}}^{(Z)}\pi_{LT}+\mu^{-1}{\vphantom{\pi}}{}^{(Z)}\pi_{TT})(\mathring{L}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m})+{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{\underline{L}}X}(\not{d}^{X}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m})-\frac{1}{2}(\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi})\mathring{\underline{L}}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m},\\
&\mu{{\vphantom{\not{C}}}{}^{(Z)}\not{C}_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,X}=-\frac{1}{2}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{\underline{L}}X}(\mathring{L}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m})-\frac{1}{2}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}X}(\mathring{\underline{L}}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m})+{\vphantom{{\pi}}}^{(Z)}{\pi}_{\mathring{L}T}(\not{d}_{X}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m})+\frac{1}{2}\mu\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}\not{d}_{X}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}.\end{split}

(3.95)

Substituting (
3.95
) into (
3.94
) directly would lead to a lengthy and tedious equation for
\mu\mathscr{D}^{\alpha}{{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z)}C_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,\alpha}
. To overcome this default and handle the resulting terms more easily,
one can decompose
\mu\mathscr{D}^{\alpha}{{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z)}C_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,\alpha}
into the following three parts as in
[
22
]
:

\mu\mathscr{D}^{\alpha}{{\vphantom{C}}{}^{(Z)}C_{\gamma}^{m}}_{,\alpha}={\vphantom{D}}^{(Z)}D_{\gamma,1}^{m}+{\vphantom{D}}^{(Z)}D_{\gamma,2}^{m}+{\vphantom{D}}^{(Z)}D_{\gamma,3}^{m},

(3.96)

where

\begin{split}{\vphantom{D}}{}^{(Z)}D_{\gamma,1}^{m}=&\frac{1}{2}\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}\big(\mathring{L}\mathring{\underline{L}}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}+\frac{1}{2}\textrm{tr}{\chi}\mathring{\underline{L}}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}\big)-{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{\underline{L}}X}(\not{d}^{X}\mathring{L}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m})-{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}X}(\not{d}^{X}\mathring{\underline{L}}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m})\\
&+({\vphantom{\pi}}^{(Z)}\pi_{\mathring{L}T}+{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(Z)}\pi_{\tilde{T}T})(\mathring{L}^{2}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m})+\frac{1}{2}\mu\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{{\not{\pi}}}}^{(Z)}{\not{\pi}}\not{\triangle}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}+{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(Z)}\pi_{\mathring{L}T}\not{\triangle}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m},\end{split}

(3.97)

\begin{split}{\vphantom{D}}{}^{(Z)}D_{\gamma,2}^{m}=&\big\{\mathring{L}({\vphantom{\pi}}^{(Z)}\pi_{\mathring{L}T}+{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(Z)}\pi_{\tilde{T}T})-\frac{1}{2}\not{\nabla}^{X}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{\underline{L}}X}+\frac{1}{4}\mathring{\underline{L}}(\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi})\big\}\mathring{L}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}+\frac{1}{4}\mathring{L}(\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi})\mathring{\underline{L}}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}\\
&-\big\{\frac{1}{2}\not{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathring{\underline{L}}}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}X}-\not{d}_{X}{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(Z)}\pi_{\mathring{L}T}-\frac{1}{2}\not{d}_{X}(\mu\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi})+\frac{1}{2}(\not{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathring{L}}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{\underline{L}}X})\big\}\not{d}^{X}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}\\
&-\frac{1}{2}(\not{\nabla}^{X}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}X})\mathring{\underline{L}}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m},\end{split}

(3.98)

\begin{split}{\vphantom{D}}{}^{(Z)}D_{\gamma,3}^{m}=&\frac{1}{2}\Big\{(\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi})(\not{d}_{X}\mu+2\mu\zeta_{X})-(\mathring{L}\mu-\mu\textrm{tr}\chi-\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(T)}\not{\pi}){\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}X}+\textrm{tr}{\chi}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{\underline{L}}X}\Big\}\not{d}^{X}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}\\
&+\Big\{\textrm{tr}{\chi}({\vphantom{\pi}}^{(Z)}\pi_{\mathring{L}T}+{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(Z)}\pi_{\tilde{T}T})+\frac{1}{4}(\mu\textrm{tr}{\chi}+\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(T)}\not{\pi})\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}+\frac{1}{2}\not{d}^{X}\mu{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}X}\Big\}\mathring{L}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}.\end{split}

(3.99)

Note that all the terms in
{\vphantom{D}}{}^{(Z)}D_{\gamma,1}^{m}
are the products of the deformation tensor and the second
order derivatives of
\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}
, except the first term containing the factor of the form
\mathring{L}\mathring{\underline{L}}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}+\frac{1}{2}\textrm{tr}{\chi}\mathring{\underline{L}}\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}
(see (
3.97
)). It should be emphasized here that such a structure is crucial in our analysis since
\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}
is the derivative of
\varphi_{\gamma}
and by (
2.64
),
\mathring{L}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}\textrm{tr}{\chi}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}=H_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}
admits the better smallness and the faster time decay than those for
\mathring{L}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}
and
\frac{1}{2}\textrm{tr}{\chi}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}
separately.
{\vphantom{D}}{}^{(Z)}D_{\gamma,2}^{m}

collects all the products of the first order derivatives of the deformation
tensor and the first order derivatives of
\Psi_{\gamma}^{m}
, and
{\vphantom{D}}{}^{(Z)}D_{\gamma,3}^{m}
is the rest.

The explicit expressions of
\Phi_{\gamma}^{m+1}
given in (
3.92
)-(
3.93
) and (
3.96
)-(
3.99
) are important to estimate the last two integrals of (
3.89
).
From the left side of (
3.89
), it is natural to define the corresponding weighted energies and fluxes as in
[
22
]
:

\displaystyle E_{i,m+1}(s,u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma=0}^{4}\sum_{Z\in\{\varrho\mathring{L},T,R\}}\delta^{2l}E_{i}[Z^{m}\varphi_{\gamma}](s,u),\quad i=1,2,

(3.100)

\displaystyle F_{i,m+1}(s,u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma=0}^{4}\sum_{Z\in\{\varrho\mathring{L},T,R\}}\delta^{2l}F_{i}[Z^{m}\varphi_{\gamma}](s,u),\quad i=1,2,

(3.101)

\displaystyle E_{i,\leq m+1}(s,u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq n\leq m}E_{i,n+1}(s,u),\quad i=1,2,

(3.102)

\displaystyle F_{i,\leq m+1}(s,u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq n\leq m}F_{i,n+1}(s,u),\quad i=1,2,

(3.103)

where
l
is the number of
T
in
Z^{m}
. These weighted energies will be estimated in the subsequent sections.

Next, one derives the higher order
L^{2}
estimates for some related quantities
so that the last two terms of (
3.89
) can be absorbed by the left hand side, and hence the higher order energy estimates on (
2.49
) can
be obtained. To this end, on needs two lemmas in
[
10
, Section 6]
, where analogous results in 3D case
can be found in
[
22
, Lemma 7.3]
and
[
23
, Lemma 12.57]
respectively.

Lemma 3.1
.

For any function
\psi\in C^{1}(D^{s,u})
vanishing on
C_{0}
, one has that for small
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{S_{s,u}}\psi^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta\int_{\Sigma_{s}^{u}}|T\psi|^{2}\lesssim\delta\int_{\Sigma_{s}^{u}}\big(|\mathring{\underline{L}}\psi|^{2}+\mu^{2}|\mathring{L}\psi|^{2}\big),

(3.104)

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{s}^{u}}\psi^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{2}\int_{\Sigma_{s}^{u}}|T\psi|^{2}\lesssim\delta^{2}\int_{\Sigma_{s}^{u}}\big(|\mathring{\underline{L}}\psi|^{2}+\mu^{2}|\mathring{L}\psi|^{2}\big).

(3.105)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{S_{s,u}}\psi^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta\big(E_{2}[\psi](s,u)+\varrho^{-2\iota}E_{1}[\psi](s,u)\big),

(3.106)

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{s}^{u}}\psi^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{2}\big(E_{2}[\psi](s,u)+\varrho^{-2\iota}E_{1}[\psi](s,u)\big).

(3.107)

Lemma 3.2
.

For any function
f\in C(D^{s,u})
, set

F(s,u,\vartheta):=\int_{t_{0}}^{s}f(\tau,u,\vartheta)d\tau.

Under the assumptions
(\star)
, then for small
\delta>0
,
